\kappa(\kappa-1)
inner vertices. It follows that the total degree of the inner vertices is at most
\kappa(\kappa-1)+2\kappa(\kappa-1)
, since the degree and branch degree differs by at most
2
. Let
B_{\mathcal{F}}
denote the vertices of
\partial\mathcal{F}
, and let
\hat{B}_{\mathcal{F}}
denote those vertices that are adjacent to some inner vertex of
\mathcal{F}
in
H^{\mathcal{F}}
. Since the total degree of inner vertices is at most
3\kappa(\kappa-1)
, this also bounds the number of vertices in
\hat{B}_{\mathcal{F}}
: we have
|\hat{B}_{\mathcal{F}}|\leq 3\kappa(\kappa-1)
.

By Lemma
3.6
we know that
\mathrm{Skel}
has
O(k)
faces, thus the sum of the branch degrees of all relevant faces is
m=O(\kappa^{2}k)
. We claim that the total branch degree of
H
is at most
O(k)
larger than
3m
. Notice that the difference between the two amounts is due to the vertices of
H
that lie on the shared boundary of some relevant faces of
\mathrm{Skel}
. Suppose now that
v
has degree
2
in
\mathrm{Skel}
with neighboring relevant faces
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
. Then
d^{*}_{H}(v)\leq d^{*}_{H^{\mathcal{F}}}(v)+d^{*}_{H^{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}}(v)+2
. Therefore, the total contribution from vertices in
\hat{B}_{\mathcal{F}}
is at most
2\kappa(\kappa-1)
, and all other vertices have all adjacent edges in the other face, so they satisfy
d^{*}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}(v)=d^{*}_{H}(v)
.

Suppose now that
v
has degree
d_{v}\geq 3
in
\mathrm{Skel}
, with neighboring faces
\mathcal{F}_{1},\dots\mathcal{F}_{d_{v}}
and corresponding branch degrees
d^{*}_{1}(v),\dots,d^{*}_{d_{v}}(v)
. Then we have

d^{*}_{H}(v)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d_{v}}d^{*}_{i}(v)+2(d_{v}-1).

Consequently, the branch degree of
\mathcal{H}
differs by at most
2\sum_{v}(d_{v}-1)
, where the sum goes over vertices of
\mathrm{Skel}
of degree at least
3
. Note that
\mathrm{Skel}
is a plane graph with
O(k)
faces, thus the sum is at most
O(k)
, concluding the proof.
∎

We say that a directed edge
uv
enters (resp. exits) a path
P
if
u\not\in V(P)
and
v\in P
(resp.
u\in V(P)
and
v\not\in V(P)
).

Lemma 3.12
.

Suppose that
P
and
P^{\prime}
are essential paths for the edges
e,e^{\prime}
respectively, and that the directed paths
Q_{s},Q,Q_{t}
do not contain these edges. Suppose that
P
and
P^{\prime}
exit the directed path
Q_{s}
before
e
and
e^{\prime}
respectively, and the edges
e,e^{\prime}
enter the same directed path
Q
. Alternatively, suppose that
e,e^{\prime}
exit the same directed path
Q
, and after them
P
and
P^{\prime}
enter the same directed path
Q_{t}
. Then
P
and
P^{\prime}
are vertex-disjoint, and the paths
Q_{s},Q
(respectively,
Q,Q_{t}
) have the same “direction”, that is, their intersections with
P
and
P^{\prime}
appear in the same order.

Proof.

Let
e=uv
and
f=u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
, and let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be essential paths through
e
and
f
respectively, and suppose that
uv
and
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
both enter a directed path. Let
Q
denote the directed path from
v
to
v^{\prime}
. Let
s,t
and
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}
denote the start- and endpoints of
P
and
P^{\prime}
, respectively. We observe that the directed path
Q_{s}
must be oriented from
s
to
s^{\prime}
: indeed, if it is oriented the other way, then
P^{\prime}
cannot be an essential path for
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
, as we can use the path
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{Q_{s}}s\xrightarrow{P}v\xrightarrow{Q}v^{\prime}
instead of
P^{\prime}[s^{\prime},v^{\prime}]
, and we claim that this path avoids
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
. In case of
Q_{s}
and
Q
do not contain
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
, so one only needs to check
P
. But if
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in P[s,v]
, then we could create closed walk together with
Q
and contradict acyclicity.

Now suppose for the sake of contradiction that
P
and
P^{\prime}
intersect at some vertex
p
. We distinguish three cases:

Case 1:

p
occurs after the edge
uv
on
P
.

This contradicts the essentiality of
uv
, as one can use
s\xrightarrow{Q_{s}}s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}}p
instead of
P[s,p]
.

Case 2:

p
occurs before the edge
uv
on
P
, and before the edge
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
on
P^{\prime}
.

This contradicts the essentiality of
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
, as one can use
p\xrightarrow{P}v\xrightarrow{Q}v^{\prime}
instead of
P^{\prime}[p,v^{\prime}]
.

Case 3:

p
occurs before the edge
uv
on
P
, but after the edge
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
on
P^{\prime}
.

This contradicts the acyclicity of
H
, as
p\xrightarrow{P}v\xrightarrow{Q}v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}}p
is a closed walk.

Suppose now that
uv
and
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
both exit a directed path. Let
Q
denote the directed path from
u
to
u^{\prime}
. We observe that the directed path
Q_{t}
must be oriented from
t
to
t^{\prime}
: indeed, if it is oriented the other way, then
P
cannot be an essential path for
uv
, as we can use the path
u\xrightarrow{Q}u^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}}t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{Q_{t}}t
instead of
P[u,t]
. Again this path avoids
uv
as if
uv\in P^{\prime}[u^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
then together with
Q
we would get a closed walk.

Now suppose for the sake of contradiction that
P
and
P^{\prime}
intersect at some vertex
p
. We distinguish three cases:

Case 1:

p
occurs before the edge
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
on
P^{\prime}
.

This contradicts the essentiality of
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
, as one can use
p\xrightarrow{P}t\xrightarrow{Q_{t}}t^{\prime}
instead of
P^{\prime}[p,t^{\prime}]
.

Case 2:

p
occurs after the edge
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
on
P^{\prime}
, and after the edge
uv
on
P
.

This contradicts the essentiality of
uv
, as one can use
u\xrightarrow{Q}u^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}}p
instead of
P[u,p]
.

Case 3:

p
occurs after the edge
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}
on
P^{\prime}
, but before the edge
uv
on
P
.

This contradicts the acyclicity of
H
, as
p\xrightarrow{P}u\xrightarrow{Q}u^{\prime}\xrightarrow{P^{\prime}}p
is a closed walk.

This concludes the proof.
∎

A
bundle
is a collection of pairwise vertex-disjoint essential paths of
\mathcal{F}
that exit a directed path
P_{s}
of
\partial\mathcal{F}
and enter a directed path
P_{t}
of
\partial\mathcal{F}
. For a cycle
C
in
\bar{H}^{\mathcal{F}}_{\Delta}
let
H^{C}
denote the edges of
H
that are inside
2
2

2

That is, in the fixed embedding, these edges lie entirely in the bounded region defined by
C
. Recall that all relevant faces of
\mathrm{Skel}
are bounded.

C
or on
C
.

Definition 3.13

(Grid structure)
.

A grid structure of size
\lambda
is a cycle
C
in
\bar{H}^{\mathcal{F}}_{\Delta}
and a pair of directed path sets
\mathcal{B}_{P}=\{P_{1},\dots,P_{\lambda}\}
and
\mathcal{B}_{Q}=\{Q_{1},\dots,Q_{\lambda}\}
, where the paths are in
H^{C}
, and the following properties hold. See Figure
8
for an illustration.

(i)

Path
P_{i}
starts at
s^{P}_{i}\in V(C)
and ends at
t^{P}_{i}\in V(C)
. Similarly, path
Q_{j}
starts at some
s^{Q}_{j}\in V(C)
and ends at
t^{Q}_{j}\in V(C)
. Apart from their start- and endpoints, each
P_{i}
and
Q_{j}
is vertex-disjoint from
C
. The paths of
\mathcal{B}_{P}
are pairwise vertex-disjoint, and the paths of
\mathcal{B}_{Q}
are pairwise vertex disjoint.

(ii)

The cycle
C
contains the vertices

s^{P}_{1},s^{P}_{2},\dots,s^{P}_{\lambda},\quad t^{Q}_{1},t^{Q}_{2},\dots,t^{Q}_{\lambda},\quad t^{P}_{\lambda},t^{P}_{\lambda-1},\dots,t^{P}_{1},\quad s^{Q}_{\lambda},s^{Q}_{\lambda-1},\dots,s^{Q}_{1}

in this cyclic order, or reversed.

(iii)

There is a directed path
Q_{s}\subset C
through
s^{P}_{1},s^{P}_{2},\dots,s^{P}_{\lambda}
. Similarly, the directed path
Q_{t}\subset C
goes through
t^{P}_{1},t^{P}_{2},\dots,t^{P}_{\lambda}
, the path
P_{s}\subset C
goes through
s^{Q}_{1},s^{Q}_{2},\dots s^{Q}_{\lambda}
, and the path
P_{t}\subset C
goes through
t^{Q}_{1},t^{Q}_{2},\dots,t^{Q}_{\lambda}
.

(iv)

Each
P_{i}
intersects each
Q_{j}
in some non-empty connected subpath.

(v)

Each
P_{i}
is a subpath of an essential path for some edge
e_{i}\in P_{i}
, and each
Q_{j}
is a subpath of an essential path for some edge
e^{\prime}_{j}\in Q_{j}
. Moreover,
e_{i}\not\in Q_{j}
and
e^{\prime}_{j}\not\in Q_{i}
for any
i,j\in[\lambda]
.

(vi)

For each
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq\lambda
the head of
e_{i_{2}}
is reachable from the tail of
e_{i_{1}}
within
H^{C}
using a path that avoids
e_{i_{1}}
or
e_{i_{2}}
. Similarly, the head of
e^{\prime}_{i_{2}}
is reachable from the tail of
e^{\prime}_{i_{1}}
within
H^{C}
using a path that avoids
e^{\prime}_{i_{1}}
or
e^{\prime}_{i_{2}}
.

Figure 8
:
A grid structure with cycle
C
and path sets
\mathcal{B}_{P},\mathcal{B}_{Q}
. Thick edges are the defining essential edges.

Let us fix a vertex
x_{i,j}\in P_{i}\cap Q_{j}
for each
i,j\in[\lambda]
. The embedding ensures that
x_{i,1},\dots,x_{i,\lambda}
appear in this order on
P_{i}
, and
x_{1,j},\dots,x_{\lambda,j}
appear in this order on
Q_{j}
. Observe that contracting the edges of
P_{i}\cap Q_{j}
as well as all edges that have an incident degree-
2
edge results in a plane grid with grid lines
P_{i}
and
Q_{j}
.

Our task now is to find a large grid structure. Consider a bundle
\mathcal{B}
consisting of paths
P_{1},\dots,P_{t}
from long segment
A
to long segment
B
where the indices are according to the order of starting points on
A
. Suppose that
P_{t/4}
starts at
a_{1}\in A
and ends in
b_{1}\in B
, and that
P_{3t/4}
starts at
a_{2}\in A
and ends in
b_{2}\in B
. The
spread
of
\mathcal{B}
is defined as
\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{A}}(a_{1},a_{2})+\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{B}}(b_{1},b_{2})
.

Lemma 3.14
.

Suppose that
\kappa>L^{4}
and that
L
is large enough. If
H\setminus\mathrm{int}\mathcal{F}
fails
\kappa
demands, then
H^{\mathcal{F}}
has a grid structure of size
\Omega(L)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
we have that each vertex on
\partial\mathcal{F}
has degree
O(Lc_{\mathrm{Skel}})
. Since there are at most
Lc_{\mathrm{Skel}}
vertices on
\partial\mathcal{F}
that are on some short segment, we have that at least
\kappa-O(L^{2}c^{2}_{\mathrm{Skel}})
demands whose essential paths go through long segments. Consequently, there exists a long segment
S
that has
\frac{\kappa-O(L^{2}c^{2}_{\mathrm{Skel}})}{c_{\mathrm{Skel}}}
demands going through it. At least half of these demands are exiting
S
or entering
S
; suppose the former, i.e., there are at least
\frac{\kappa-O(L^{2}c^{2}_{\mathrm{Skel}})}{2c_{\mathrm{Skel}}}
demands exiting
S
(the entering case can be handled analogously). Since vertices of segment
S
have degree at most
O(Lc_{\mathrm{Skel}})
, we have that among these demands exiting
S
, there must be at least
\frac{\kappa-O(L^{2}c^{2}_{\mathrm{Skel}})}{2c_{\mathrm{Skel}}\cdot O(Lc_{\mathrm{Skel}})}=\Omega(\kappa/L)
demands exiting
S
that have pairwise distinct starting vertices.

Each of these demands have some edge exiting
S
, and for each of these edges let us fix a corresponding essential path. Among these essential paths, at most
O(L^{2}c^{2}_{\mathrm{Skel}})
of them end on short segments, and among the rest, at least
\frac{1}{c_{\mathrm{Skel}}}
proportion of them end on the same long segment of
\partial\mathcal{F}
. Thus there are at least

\frac{\Omega(\kappa/L)-O(L^{2}c^{2}_{\mathrm{Skel}})}{c_{\mathrm{Skel}}}=\Omega(\kappa/L)

essential paths among them that also end on the same long segment of
\partial\mathcal{F}
. Among these paths, at least
\frac{1}{O(Lc_{\mathrm{Skel}})}
proportion of them have pairwise distinct ending points. Thus, there exists a bundle of size at least
\Omega(\kappa/L^{2})
where either all defining essential edges exit a long segment of
\partial\mathcal{F}
, or all defining essential edges enter a long segment of
\partial\mathcal{F}
. We fix a constant
c^{*}
such that there exists a bundle of size at least
c^{*}\kappa/L^{2}
. Let
\mathcal{B}
be the bundle of minimum spread that has exactly
\lambda:=\lfloor L/(4c^{2}_{\mathrm{Skel}})\rfloor
and where the defining edges are all entering or all exiting a long segment. Since
L
is large enough, we may assume
L>1/c^{*}
, so
c^{*}\kappa/L^{2}>\kappa/L^{3}>L>\lambda
, so such a bundle
\mathcal{B}
exists.

By Lemma
3.12
, we have that the paths of
\mathcal{B}
are pairwise vertex-disjoint. Moreover, by the same lemma, we can index the paths of
\mathcal{B}
by the order in which their starting points occur in
\partial\mathcal{F}
as
P_{1},\dots,P_{\lambda}
. By the essentiality of the defining edges of
\mathcal{B}
, we know that the target of a path cannot be reachable from its source, thus the starting and ending long segments of
\mathcal{B}
are distinct and they cannot be subpaths of the same directed path of
H
. Thus Lemma
3.6
implies that the starting and ending long segment of
\mathcal{B}
are distant, therefore there are at least
L
vertices on the middle path
P_{m}
of
\mathcal{B}
, where we set
m=\lfloor\lambda/2\rfloor
. Note that each vertex of
P_{m}
has degree at least
3
, thus each of these vertices has an incident edge that is not on
P_{m}
. At least half of these edges are on the same side of
P_{m}
in the embedding, and at least half of them are all entering or all exiting
P_{m}
. Consider now the essential paths of the
L/4
edges selected this way. At least
1/c^{2}_{\mathrm{Skel}}
proportion of them have the same starting and ending segment, thus there are at least
\lambda=\lfloor L/(4c^{2}_{\mathrm{Skel}})\rfloor
paths all entering or all exiting
P_{m}
in the same direction. Let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be this size-
\lambda
collection of essential paths.

We can index the paths of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
by the order in which their essential edge occur on
P_{m}
as
P^{\prime}_{1},\dots,P^{\prime}_{\lambda}
. Let
e^{\prime}_{j}
the essential edge of
P^{\prime}_{j}
.

Let
S_{\mathcal{B}},T_{\mathcal{B}},S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}},T^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
be starting and ending segments of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, respectively. We denote by
e_{i}
and
e^{\prime}_{i}
the essential edge of
P_{i}
and
P^{\prime}_{i}
. Suppose now that
P_{i}
and
P^{\prime}_{j}
intersect more than once, and let
x
and
x^{\prime}
be the first and last intersection along
P_{i}
. By acyclicity of
H
, we know that
x
and
x^{\prime}
are also the first and last intersection along
P^{\prime}_{j}
. Observe that the
e_{i}
and
e^{\prime}_{j}
cannot occur between
x
and
x^{\prime}
, as that would allow us to circumnavigate an essential edge using a portion of the other path. Consequently, we can change
P^{\prime}_{j}
by exchanging
P^{\prime}_{j}[x,x^{\prime}]
with
P_{i}[x,x^{\prime}]
; the result is still an essential path for the edge
e^{\prime}_{j}
. By making such changes exhaustively, we can ensure that for each
i,j\in[\lambda]
if
P_{i}
intersects
P_{j}
, then their intersection is a connected (possibly one-vertex) subpath.

We now distinguish several cases based on what segment
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
starts and ends on. Note that
S_{\mathcal{B}}\neq T_{\mathcal{B}}
and
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}\neq T^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
by essentiality of
e_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{1}
, and acyclicity. Assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{B}
goes from bottom to top, with both
S_{\mathcal{B}}
and
T_{\mathcal{B}}
oriented left to right. See Figure
9
for an illustration.

Case 1.

S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}\not\in\{S_{\mathcal{B}},T_{\mathcal{B}}\}
(or symmetrically,
T^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}\not\in\{S_{\mathcal{B}},T_{\mathcal{B}}\}
).
First we show that the paths of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
are pairwise disjoint. Suppose that the edges
e^{\prime}_{j}
are entering
P_{m}
from the left. Then we can apply Lemma
3.12
with
P_{1}
playing the role of
Q_{s}
and
P_{m}
playing the role of
Q
. If they are exiting
P_{m}
on the right, and
T^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}\not\in\{S_{\mathcal{B}},T_{\mathcal{B}}\}
, then Lemma
3.12
is applied with
Q=P_{m}
and
Q_{t}=P_{\lambda}
. If
T^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}\in\{S_{\mathcal{B}},T_{\mathcal{B}}\}
, then Lemma
3.12
is applied with
Q=P_{m}
and
Q_{t}=T^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
. All remaining cases can be handled with analogous invocations of Lemma
3.12
.

Suppose that the edges
e^{\prime}_{i}
enter
P_{m}
from the left or exit it on the right. Because of the embedding it follows that all paths of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
intersect
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{m}
. We claim that if the edges
e^{\prime}_{i}
exit
P_{m}
, then the paths
P^{\prime}_{i}
also intersect the path
Z=S_{\mathcal{B}}[m,m+1]\cup P_{m+1}
, where
S_{\mathcal{B}}[m,m+1]
denotes the portion of
S_{\mathcal{B}}
between the starting point of
P_{m}
and
P_{m+1}
. Note that
P^{\prime}_{i}
enters the inside of the closed curve
P_{m}\cup S_{\mathcal{B}}[m,m+1]\cup P_{m+1}\cup T_{\mathcal{B}}[m,m+1]
, so
P^{\prime}_{i}
must intersect
Z
or
T_{\mathcal{B}}[m,m+1]
after passing
e^{\prime}_{i}
(because of the earlier simplifaction it cannot intersect
P_{m}
again). If
P^{\prime}_{i}
enters
T_{\mathcal{B}}[m,m+1]
, then its portion containing
e^{\prime}_{i}
can be curcumnavigated on a directed subpath of
P_{m}\cup T_{\mathcal{B}}[m,m+1]
, contradicting the essentaility of
e^{\prime}_{i}
.

If
e^{\prime}_{i}
enters
P_{m}
, then we set
Z=P_{m}
.

Consider the cycle
C
of
\bar{H}
formed by
P_{1},P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{m},Z
(Since
P_{1},Z
and
P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{m}
are vertex-disjoint, and the other pairs have a connected intersection, there is a unique cycle in the union
P_{1}\cup P^{\prime}_{1}\cup P^{\prime}_{m}\cup Z
.) One can verify that
C
,
\{P_{2}|_{C},\dots,P_{m-1}|_{C}\}
, and
\{P^{\prime}_{2}|_{C},\dots,P^{\prime}_{m-1}|_{C}\}
form a grid structure, where
P|_{C}
denotes the portion of the path
P
that falls in the interior of the bounded region of
C
.

The case when the edges
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
enter
P_{m}
from the right or exit it to the left can be handled analogously, using the paths
P_{m},\dots,P_{\lambda}
instead of
P_{1},\dots,P_{m}
, and setting
Z=P_{m-1}\cup T_{\mathcal{B}}[m-1,m]
or
Z=P_{m}
for entering/exiting edges
e^{\prime}_{i}
. The cycle
C
is defined by
Z,P_{\lambda},P^{\prime}_{m},P^{\prime}_{\lambda}
, and the grid is given by
C
,
\{P_{m+1}|_{C},\dots,P_{\lambda-1}|_{C}\}
, and
\{P^{\prime}_{m+1}|_{C},\dots,P^{\prime}_{\lambda-1}|_{C}\}
. In all cases the grid has size at least
\lfloor\lambda/2\rfloor-2
.

Figure 9
:
Finding a grid structure based on a bundle
\mathcal{B}
(orange). The edges
e^{\prime}_{j}
exit the middle path
P_{m}
on the right. The bundle
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
(red) has a distinct starting segment
S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
, as in Case 1. The directed path
Z
is depicted with dashed line, and the cycle
C
around the found grid is the boundary of the gray shaded region.

Case 2.

S_{\mathcal{B}}=S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
T_{\mathcal{B}}=T^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
. We claim that this case cannot occur.
If
e^{\prime}_{i}
exits
P_{m}
on the right, then we can exchange the portion of
P^{\prime}_{i}
starting at
e^{\prime}_{i}
with a part of
P_{m}
and
T_{\mathcal{B}}
, contradicting the essentiality of
e^{\prime}_{i}
. If
e^{\prime}_{i}
enters
P_{m}
from the left, then we can exchange the portion of
P^{\prime}_{i}
ending at
e^{\prime}_{i}
with a part of
S_{\mathcal{B}}
and
P_{m}
, contradicting the essentiality of
e^{\prime}_{i}
.

If
e^{\prime}_{i}
exits
P_{m}
on the left, or enters
P_{m}
from the right, then we can exchange the portion of
P_{m}
starting at
e_{m}
with a part of
S_{\mathcal{B}}
and
P^{\prime}_{m}
, contradicting the essentiality of
e_{m}
.

Case 3.

S_{\mathcal{B}}=T^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
T_{\mathcal{B}}=S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
.
First we note that
e^{\prime}_{i}
cannot enter
P_{m}
from the right or exit it on the left, as both would create a cycle. We can invoke Lemma
3.12
on any pair of paths of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
with
Q=P_{m}
and either
Q_{s}=S^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
or
Q_{t}=T^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}
to prove that the paths of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
are pairwise vertex-disjoint.

Since
\mathcal{B}
has minimum spread, we have that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
has a spread at least as big. Recall that if
\mathcal{B}
goes from
A=S_{\mathcal{B}}
to
B=T_{\mathcal{B}}
, then
P_{\lambda/4}
starts at
a_{1}\in A
and ends in
b_{1}\in B
, while
P_{3\lambda/4}
starts at
a_{2}\in A
and ends in
b_{2}\in B
. Now
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
goes from
B
to
A
, so we set the start and endpoint of
P^{\prime}_{\lambda/4}
as
b^{\prime}_{1}\in B
and
a^{\prime}_{1}\in A
, and similarly, the start and end of
P^{\prime}_{3\lambda/4}
as
b^{\prime}_{2}\in B
and
a^{\prime}_{2}\in A
. Now
\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{A}}(a_{1},a_{2})+\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{B}}(b_{1},b_{2})\leq\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{A}}(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2})+\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{B}}(b^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{2})
, thus at least one of the inequalities
\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{A}}(a_{1},a_{2})\leq\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{A}}(b^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{2})
and
\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{B}}(b_{1},b_{2})\leq\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{B}}(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2})
holds.

Suppose that the latter inequality holds. Then because of the embedding we have that
P^{\prime}_{1},\dots,P^{\prime}_{\lfloor\lambda/4\rfloor}
all intersect
P_{\lceil\lambda/4\rceil},\dots,P_{\lfloor\lambda/2\rfloor}
. We can then define a grid for these two smaller bundles of size at least
\lambda/4-2
as seen in Case 1 by imagining that the path
T_{\mathcal{B}}
is split into two shorter paths, the first part containing the endpoints of
P^{\prime}_{1},\dots,P^{\prime}_{\lfloor\lambda/4\rfloor}
, and the second containing the starting points of
P_{\lceil\lambda/4\rceil},\dots,P_{\lfloor\lambda/2\rfloor}
. Similarly, if the former inequality (
\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{B}}(b_{1},b_{2})\leq\mathrm{dist}_{\bar{B}}(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2})
) holds, then
P^{\prime}_{\lceil 3\lambda/4\rceil},\dots,P^{\prime}_{\lambda}
all intersect
P_{\lceil\lambda/2\rceil},\dots,P_{\lfloor 3\lambda/4\rfloor}
, and the grid can again be defined analogously to Case 1.

In all possible cases we have shown that a grid of size
\Omega(\lambda)=\Omega(L)
exists which completes the proof.
∎

The usefulness of the grid structure is demonstrated by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.15
.

If
C,\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
form a grid structure of size
\lambda
, then the demands corresponding to a subset of their essential paths form a
(\lambda-2)
-tough pair.

Proof.

First we show that the demands corresponding to the essential paths
\mathcal{B}_{P}=\{P_{1},\dots,P_{\lambda}\}
are pairwise weakly independent. For a path
P_{i}
let
d^{P}_{i}=u^{P}_{i}v^{P}_{i}
be the corresponding minimal demand, which is served by some path
P^{ex}_{i}
that is an extension of
P_{i}
. (Similarly, the minimal demand of
Q_{j}
is
d^{Q}_{j}=u^{Q}_{j}v^{Q}_{j}
, and it is served by the path
Q^{ex}_{j}
.) See Figure
10
for an illustration.

Suppose now that
d^{P}_{i}
and
d^{P}_{j}
are not weakly independent, where
i<j
. There cannot be a demand
v^{P}_{j}u^{P}_{i}
, as a path satisfying this demand together with
P^{ex}_{i}[u^{P}_{i},x_{i,1}]\cup Q_{1}[x_{i,1},x_{j,1}]\cup P^{ex}_{j}[x_{j,1},v^{P}_{j}]
forms a closed walk, i.e., contradicts acyclicity. Thus weak independence must be violated by a demand
v^{P}_{i}u^{P}_{j}
; let
P_{d}
be the path corresponding to this demand. We claim that this contradicts the essentiality of
e^{\prime}_{2}
, as
Q^{*}=Q_{2}[s^{Q}_{2},t^{Q}_{2}]
can be exchanged with the path

P^{*}=s^{Q}_{2}\xrightarrow{P_{s}}s^{Q}_{3}\xrightarrow{Q_{3}}x_{i,3}\xrightarrow{P^{ex}_{i}}v^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{P_{d}}u^{P}_{j}\xrightarrow{P^{ex}_{j}}x_{j,1}\xrightarrow{Q_{1}}t^{Q}_{1}\xrightarrow{P_{t}}t^{Q}_{2}.

Figure 10
:
(i) Proving weak independence: a demand
v^{P}_{i}u^{P}_{j}
leads to the path
Q_{2}
being avoidable, contradicting essentiality of
e^{\prime}_{2}
. (ii) Proving strong independence. A demand
u^{P}_{i}u^{Q}_{j}
leads to the edge
e_{i}
being avoidable on
P^{ex}_{i}
, contradicting its essentiality for
u^{P}_{i}v^{P}_{i}
. In both sides the path
P^{*}
is represented as the concatenation of the path(s) with orange background and the blue path
P_{d}
.

Unless
P^{*}
also contains
e^{\prime}_{2}
, this exchange contradicts the essentiality of
e^{\prime}_{2}
.
Suppose now that
P^{*}
does contain
e^{\prime}_{2}
; by the properties of the grid we know that
e^{\prime}_{2}
cannot lie on any of
P_{s},Q_{3},P^{ex}_{i},P^{ex}_{j},Q_{1},P_{t}
, thus it could only be contained in
P_{d}
. Notice that if
e^{\prime}_{2}
is not essential for the demand of
P_{d}
, then we can change
P_{d}
to exclude
e^{\prime}_{2}
and get a contradiction as above. Thus in what follows, we assume that
e^{\prime}_{2}
is an essential edge for
P_{d}
.

Notice that the same argument can be repeated for
e^{\prime}_{3}
, thus
P_{d}
must contain
e^{\prime}_{3}
as an essential edge. By Property (vi) of grid structures, the head of
e^{\prime}_{3}
is reachable from the tail of
e^{\prime}_{2}
. It follows that
e^{\prime}_{2},e^{\prime}_{3}
must appear in this order on
P_{d}
, as otherwise we could create a closed walk (
\text{tail}(e^{\prime}_{2})\rightarrow\text{head}(e^{\prime}_{3})\xrightarrow{P_{d}}\text{tail}(e^{\prime}_{2})
), contradicting acyclicity. Consequently,
P_{d}
has a subpath

\text{tail}(e^{\prime}_{2})\xrightarrow{e^{\prime}_{2}}\text{head}(e^{\prime}_{2})\xrightarrow{P_{d}}\text{tail}(e^{\prime}_{3})\xrightarrow{e^{\prime}_{2}}\text{head}(e^{\prime}_{3}),

which we could replace with the guaranteed path
\text{tail}(e^{\prime}_{2})\rightarrow\text{head}(e^{\prime}_{3})
from the grid structure that avoids
e^{\prime}_{2}
or
e^{\prime}_{3}
, contradicting the essentiality of either
e^{\prime}_{2}
or
e^{\prime}_{3}
for
P_{d}
.
The weak independence of the paths
Q_{j}
can be proven symmetrically (by switching the role of
P
and
Q
).

Next we show strong independence of
d^{P}_{i}
and
d^{Q}_{j}
for all
i,j\in\{2,3,\dots,\lambda-1\}
. Notice that this is sufficient, as it shows that the demands
d^{P}_{2},\dots,d^{P}_{\lambda-1}
and
d^{Q}_{2},\dots,d^{Q}_{\lambda-1}
form a
(\lambda-2)
-tough pair. Observe that having a demand
v^{Q}_{j}u^{P}_{i}
or
v^{P}_{i}u^{Q}_{j}
for any
i,j
would create a closed walk:

x_{i,j}\xrightarrow{Q^{ex}_{j}}v^{Q}_{j}\xrightarrow{}u^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{P^{ex}_{i}}x_{i,j}\quad\text{ and }\quad x_{i,j}\xrightarrow{P^{ex}_{i}}v^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{}u^{Q}_{j}\xrightarrow{Q_{j}}x_{i,j},

respectively, contradicting acyclicity.
Suppose now that there is a demand
u^{P}_{i}u^{Q}_{j}
served by a path
P_{d}
, where
i,j\in\{2,3,\dots,\lambda-1\}
. Then the path
P^{ex}_{i}[u^{P}_{i},t^{P}_{i}]
can be replaced by

P^{*}=u^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{P_{d}}u^{Q}_{j}\xrightarrow{Q^{ex}_{j}}x_{1,j}\xrightarrow{P_{1}}t^{P}_{1}\xrightarrow{Q_{t}}t^{P}_{i}.

Similarly to earlier, the existence of such a path contradicts the essentiality of
e_{i}
, unless
P^{*}
passes through
e_{i}
. The grid properties imply that
e_{i}
cannot lie on any of
Q^{ex}_{j},P_{1},Q_{t}
, thus it must lie on
P_{d}
, and moreover, that it must be essential for
P_{d}
. We now distinguish two cases based on the location of
e_{i}
on
P_{i}
.

Case 1.

Edge
e_{i}
comes before
x_{i,j}
on
P_{i}
. Then
e_{i}
is inside the bounded region of the non-directed cycle

C^{\prime}=s^{P}_{i-1}\xrightarrow{Q_{s}}s^{P}_{i+1}\xrightarrow{P_{i+1}}x_{i+1,j}\xleftarrow{Q_{j}}x_{i-1,j}\xleftarrow{P_{i-1}}s^{P}_{i-1},

see Figure
11
for an illustration.
We claim that after
P_{d}
passes through
e_{i}
, it is “trapped” inside
C^{\prime}
, i.e., it cannot pass through any vertex of
C^{\prime}
.

Entering some vertex
x
of
Q_{j}[x_{i-1,j},x_{i+1,j}]
after
e_{i}
is not possible since it creates a closed walk
x\xrightarrow{P_{d}}u^{Q}_{j}\xrightarrow{Q^{ex}_{j}}x
. Entering
Q_{s}[s^{P}_{i-1},s^{P}_{i}]
at vertex
x
would also create a closed walk:
x\xrightarrow{Q_{s}}s^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{P_{i}}\text{head}(e_{i})\xrightarrow{P_{d}}x
. Entering some vertex
x
of
P^{\#}:=s^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{Q_{s}}s^{P}_{i+1}\xrightarrow{P_{i+1}}x_{i+1,j}
contradicts the essentiality of
e_{i}
for
P_{d}
, as we can exchange
P_{d}[u^{P}_{i},x]
with
u^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{P^{ex}_{i}}s^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{P^{\#}}x
. Thus
P_{d}
has to enter some vertex
x
of
P_{i-1}[s^{P}_{i-1},x_{i-1,j}]
. If
x
appears before
e_{i-1}
on
P_{i-1}
, then we get a closed walk
\text{head}{e_{i}}\xrightarrow{P_{d}}x\xrightarrow{P_{i-1}}\text{tail}(e_{i-1})\xrightarrow{}\text{head}(e_{i})
, where the last portion of the walk is supplied by Property (vi) of grids. This contradicts acyclicity. If
x
appears after
e_{i-1}
, then
e_{i-1}
is non-essential for
P_{i-1}
, as
P_{i-1}[s^{P}_{i-1},x]
can be circumnavigated on
s^{P}_{i-1}\xrightarrow{Q_{s}}s^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{P_{i}}\text{head}(e_{i})\xrightarrow{P_{d}}x
. Thus no vertex of
C^{\prime}
can be entered by
P_{d}
after passing through
e_{i}
.

Figure 11
:
(i) The demand path
P_{d}
is trapped in the shaded region after passing through
e_{i}
, as entering its boundary is not possible. (ii) The demand path
P_{d}
cannot exit the boundary of the shaded region before it passes through
e_{i}
.

Case 2.

Edge
e_{i}
comes after
x_{i,j}
on
P_{i}
.
Then
e_{i}
is inside the bounded region of the non-directed cycle

C^{\prime}=x_{i-1,j}\xrightarrow{Q_{j}}x_{i+1,j}\xleftarrow{P_{i+1}}t^{P}_{i+1}\xleftarrow{Q_{t}}s^{P}_{i-1}\xleftarrow{P_{i-1}}x_{i-1,j}.

We claim that after
P_{d}
exits
C^{\prime}
, it cannot pass through
e_{i}
.
The case can be handled analogously to Case 1: If
P_{d}
exits from
Q_{j}[x_{i-1,j},x_{i+1,j}]
,
Q^{t}[t^{P}_{i},t^{P}_{i+1}]
, or
P_{i+1}
after
e_{i+1}
, then it creates a closed walk, contradicting acyclicity.
Exiting at vertex
x
of
P_{i+1}
before
e_{i+1}
contradicts the essentiality of
e_{i+1}
for
P_{i+1}
, as
P_{i+1}[x,t^{P}_{i+1}]
can be exchanged with
x\xrightarrow{P_{d}}\text{head}(e_{i})\xrightarrow{P_{i}}t^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{Q_{t}}t^{P}_{i+1}
. Exiting from vertex
x
of
P^{\#}:=x_{i-1,j}\xrightarrow{P_{i-1}}t^{P}_{i-1}\xrightarrow{Q_{t}}t^{P}_{i}
contradicts essentiality of
e_{i}
for
P^{ex}_{i}
, as
P^{ex}_{i}[u^{P}_{i},t^{P}_{i}]
can be exchanged with
u^{P}_{i}\xrightarrow{P_{d}}x\xrightarrow{P^{\#}}t^{P}_{i}
.

The non-existence of a demand
u^{Q}_{j}u^{P}_{i}
can be proven as above by exchanging the role of
P_{i}
and
Q_{j}
. If a demand
v^{P}_{i}v^{Q}_{j}
could exist, then in the reversed orientation graph it would be a valid demand
u^{P}_{i}u^{Q}_{j}
, contradicting the above arguments. The non-existence of demands of the form
v^{Q}_{j}v^{P}_{i}
then follows by exchanging the role of
P_{i}
and
Q_{j}
again. This concludes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove the Structure Theorem (Theorem
1.9
).

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}
is not
c
-bounded, that is, for any positive real number
\gamma
, there exists an instance
(G_{\gamma},T_{\gamma},D_{\gamma})
of
Planar
\mathcal{D}
-SN
and an optimum solution
H:=H_{\gamma}
such that the total branch degree of
H
is more than
\gamma k
, where
k=|T_{\gamma}|
.

We say that a quantity
\mu
is
\gamma
-tied if it can be lower bounded by
\mu>f(\gamma)
where
\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow\infty}f(\gamma)=\infty
. We need to show that
H
contains a tough pair whose size is
\gamma
-tied. This implies that we can find a sequence of patterns that have tough pairs whose size goes to infinity. We can use vertex identifications in these patterns on the tough pairs to get patterns with
t
-tough pairs for all positive integers
t
.

As discussed in the beginning of Section
3.2
, we may assume without loss of generality that
H
is acyclic, weakly connected, and the vertices of
H
have undirected degree at least
3
.

Note that if for each face
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathrm{Skel}
we have that
H\setminus\mathrm{int}\mathcal{F}
fails at most
\kappa
demands for some absolute constant
\kappa
, then each
H^{\mathcal{F}}
has total branch degree at most
O(\kappa^{2})k
by Lemma
3.11
, thus we have a face
\mathcal{F}
where
H\setminus\mathrm{int}\mathcal{F}
fails at least
\kappa=\Omega(\gamma^{1/2})
demands. We set
L=\lfloor\kappa^{1/3}\rfloor=\Omega(\gamma^{1/6})
(in particular, both
\kappa
and
L
are
\gamma
-tied). Now we invoke Lemma
3.14
, which gives us a grid structure of size
\lambda=\Omega(L)
, i.e., a grid structure whose size is
\gamma
-tied. Finally, we use Lemma
3.15
on this grid: we get a
(\lambda-2)
-tough pair. Since
\lambda
is
\gamma
-tied, this concludes the proof.
∎

4
Cleaning: Identifying to a
t
-hard-pattern

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.10
.

See
1.10

Towards the proof of Theorem
1.10
, we first prove Lemma
4.1
.

Lemma 4.1

(Identifying to a
t
-hard-pattern)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a class of graphs that is closed under transitive equivalence and identifying vertices. Let
D\in\mathcal{D}
and let
t
be a positive integer.
Then there exists
t^{\prime}
that depends only on
t
,
such that if
D
contains a
t^{\prime}
-tough-pair then there exists
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
that is a
t
-hard-pattern.

From Lemma
4.1
, the proof of Theorem
1.10
follows easily. We give this proof before proving Lemma
4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

We first prove the forward direction.
From Lemma
4.1
, for every positive integer
t
,
\mathcal{D}
contains a digraph from some
t
-hard-pattern, that is,
\mathcal{D}
contains a digraph from some
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. Since
\kappa
is finite,
there exists
i\in[\kappa]
such that infinitely many
digraphs of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
belong to
\mathcal{D}
. Further, since
\mathcal{D}
is closed under vertex identifications and from any member of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
every smaller member can be obtained by vertex identifications, we conclude that
\mathcal{C}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
.

For the backward direction observe from the definitions of
\{\mathcal{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}\}
, that each
t
-hard digraph of each
\mathcal{C}_{i}
contains in fact a
t
-tough-pair.
∎

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of Lemma
4.1
which is divided into five separate steps presented in Sections
4.1
,
4.2
,
4.3.1
,
4.3.2
and
4.4
, respectively. Below we give the main results of each of these sections and show how they together imply Lemma
4.1
. Before stating the results, we give some important definitions that are used throughout the section.

Definitions.

For any digraph
D
, we denote by
D^{\star}
the
transitive closure
of
D
, that is
D^{\star}
is obtained from
D
by repeatedly adding edges
(u,v)
whenever
(u,v)
is not already an edge but there is a
(u,v)
-path in
D
.

For a digraph
D
and two ordered sets
A=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}),B=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{t})\subseteq V(D)
such that
|A|=|B|
, we say that
D
has an
(A,B)
-matching
if for each
i\in[t]
,
(a_{i},b_{i})\in E(D)
. We say that
D
has an
(A,B)
-induced-matching
if
D
has an
(A,B)
-matching, for each
i\neq j
,
(a_{i},b_{j})\not\in E(D)
,
(b_{j},a_{i})\not\in E(D)
,
(b_{i},a_{i})\not\in E(D)
and
A,B
are independent sets in
D
.

We say that
D
has an
(A,B)
-biclique
if for each
i,j\in[t]
,
(a_{i},b_{j})\in E(D)
and
(b_{j},a_{i})\not\in E(D)
. We say that
D
has an
(A,B)
-induced-biclique

if
D
has an
(A,B)
-biclique and,
A,B
are independent sets in
D
. We say that
D
has a
t
-biclique
if there exists an
(A,B)
-biclique in
D
for some
A,B\subseteq V(D)
and
|A|=|B|=t
. Similarly, we say that
D
has a
t
-induced-biclique
if there exists an
(A,B)
-induced-biclique in
D
for some
A,B\subseteq V(D)
and
|A|=|B|=t
. In all these cases, we call the set of edges
\{(a_{i},b_{i}):i\in[t]\}
as the
matching edges
of
(A,B)
.

For positive integers
i,j
, let
\mathcal{R}(i,j)
denote the minimum number of vertices such that any
\mathcal{R}(i,j)
-vertex complete graph whose edges are colored with
j
colors, contains a monochromatic clique of size
i
. Let
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i)
denote the minimum integer such that any bipartite graph that has a matching of size
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(i)
, either has an induced matching of size
t
or a
t
-induced-biclique.
From Ramsey Theorem, such numbers always exists and they depend only on
i,j
, or
i
, respectively.
Recall the definitions of weakly independence, strongly independence and
t
-tough-pair from Section
2
.

Definition 4.2

(Ordered
t
-tough-pair)
.

Given a
digraph
D
,
E_{1},E_{2}\subseteq E(D^{\star})
, we say that
(E_{1},E_{2})
is an ordered
t
-tough-pair in
D
if

1.

|E_{1}|=|E_{2}|=t
,

2.

all edges in
E_{i}
are pairwise weakly independent in
D
, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, and

3.

for each
e_{1}\in E_{1}
and
e_{2}\in E_{2}
,
(e_{1},e_{2})
are strongly independent in
D
, and

there exists an ordering of the sets
\text{head}(E_{1})=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{t})
,
\text{tail}(E_{1})=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})
,
\text{head}(E_{2})=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{t})
and
\text{tail}(E_{2})=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{t})
such that

1.

for any
1\leq i<j\leq t
, there is no
(w_{j},x_{i})
-path, no
(w_{j},w_{i})
-path and no
(x_{j},x_{i})
-path in
D
,

2.

for any
1\leq i<j\leq t
, there is no
(y_{j},z_{i})
-path, no
(y_{j},y_{i})
-path and no
(z_{j},z_{i})
-path in
D
.

If
(E_{1},E_{2})
is an ordered
t
-tough-pair then we treat
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
as ordered sets such that their head sets and tail sets satisfies the above properties. We say ordered tough-pair to mean an ordered
t
-tough-pair for some
t
.

Observe that, unlike the
t
-tough-pair, if
(E_{1},E_{2})
is an ordered
t
-tough-pair in
D
, then the edges of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
may not be minimal in
D
.

Simplifying the
t
-tough-pair.

In Section
4.1
, using Ramsey arguments we show that it
D
contains a
t
-tough-pair for a large enough
t
, then it contains one of the three structures described in Lemma
4.3
.

Lemma 4.3

(Hard sub-structures)
.

Let
D
be a reachability-minimal digraph and
t
be a positive integer.
If
D
contains an
\mathcal{R}(2t,9)
-tough-pair then one of the following holds.

1.

D
contains an ordered
t
-tough-pair  or

2.

D
contains a
t
-biclique, or

3.

there exist ordered sets
A,B\subseteq V(D)
such that
|A|=|B|=t
,
D
contains an
(A,B)
-matching and
D^{\star}
contains an
(A,B)
-induced-biclique.

Note that if
D
is reachability-minimal and contains a
t
-biclique then it contains a
t
-induced-biclique.
From Lemma
4.3
one concludes that, in order to prove Lemma
4.1
, it is enough to identify one of the three structures defined in Lemma
4.3
to some
t
-hard-pattern. The first outcome of Lemma
4.3
is handled in Section
4.3
, the second outcome is handled in Section
4.4
and the third outcome is handled in Section
4.2
.

Cleaning ordered
t
-tough-pair.

In Section
4.3
we show that if the outcome of Lemma
4.3
is an ordered
t
-tough-pair, then one can identify
D
to either a
t^{\prime}
-hard-pattern or
D^{\star}
contains a
t^{\prime}
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal in
D
.
This is achieved by two rounds of cleaning: in the first round the digraph is cleaned to an intermediate structure, called a
semi-cleaned ordered
t
-tough-pair
(defined below), and in the second round this semi-cleaned ordered
t
-tough-pair is either identified to obtain a
t^{\prime}
-hard-pattern, or it can be shown that it contains a
t^{\prime}
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal.
This is formalized in Lemmas
4.5
and
4.6
which are proved in Section
4.3.1
and
4.3.1
, respectively.

Definition 4.4

(Semi-cleaned ordered
t
-tough-pair)
.

We say that a digraph
D
is a semi-cleaned ordered
t
-tough-pair, if it contains an ordered
t
-tough-pair
(A,B)
such that the vertex set of
D
contains at most two vertices, called a source
s
and a sink
t
, outside of the set
V(A\cup B)
, and
N^{-}_{D}(\texttt{s}),N^{+}_{D}(\texttt{t})=\emptyset
. (Note that from this definition
(\texttt{t},\texttt{s})\not\in E(D)
).

Lemma 4.5

(Semi-cleaning the ordered tough-pair)
.

Let
D
be a
digraph such that
D
contains an ordered
t^{2}
-tough-pair, then one can obtain
\widehat{D}
from
D
by identification, such that
\widehat{D}
is a semi-cleaned
t
-ordered tough-pair, for some function
g
that depends only on
t
.

For any positive integer
t
, let
h(t)=2\mathcal{R}(2\mathcal{R}(2\mathcal{R}(4t+2,4),4),5)
.

Lemma 4.6

(Cleaning the semi-cleaned ordered tough-pair)
.

If
D
is a semi-cleaned ordered
h(t)
-tough-pair then either,

•

D^{\star}
contains a
t
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal in
D
, or

•

D
can be identified to a digraph
\hat{D}
such that
\hat{D}
is transitively equivalent to a
t
-hard-matching-pattern.

For any positive integer
t
, let
\textnormal{{cor}}(t)=(h(t))^{2}
.
Corollary
4.7
follows from Lemmas
4.5
and
4.6
.

Corollary 4.7
.

If a digraph
D
contains an ordered
\textnormal{{cor}}(t)
-tough-pair,
then either
D
can be identified to a
t
-hard-matching-pattern, or
D^{\star}
contains a
t
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal in
D
.

If the outcome of Corollary
4.7
a
t
-hard-matching-pattern, then we are done. Otherwise, we need to clean the
t
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal,
which is what is done next.

Cleaning a minimal induced-biclique.

In Section
4.4
we show that if
D
contains a
9t
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal, then
D
can be identified to a
t
-hard-biclique-pattern. In this case, Lemma
4.1
is proved.

Lemma 4.8

(Cleaning minimal biclique)
.

For a positive integer
t
and a digraph
D
, if
D^{\star}
contains a
9t
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal in
D
, then
D
can be identified to digraph that is transitively equivalent to a
t
-hard-biclique-pattern.

Simplifying the third outcome of Lemma
4.3
.

In Section
4.2
, we show that if Lemma
4.3
outputs its third outcome, then one can contract some edges of the input digraph (and hence obtain a digraph in the same pattern class as the input, since the pattern class is closed under identification and contraction is a type of identification) such that the resulting digraph either contains an ordered
t^{\prime}
-tough-pair (which we know how to deal using Corollary
4.7
), or it contains a
t^{\prime}
-biclique whose edges are minimal (which we also know to deal with because of Lemma
4.8
).

For positive integers
t,p
, define
f(t,p)=2^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(2t)}\cdot f(t,p-1)+\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(2t)
when
p>1
and
f(t,1)=t
.

Lemma 4.9

(Simplifying a biclique)
.

Let
D
be a directed graph and
t
be a positive integer.
Suppose there exists ordered sets

A,B\subseteq V(D)
such that
|A|=|B|\geq f(t,2t+2)
,

D^{\star}
contains an
(A,B)
-induced-biclique and the matching edges of
(A,B)
are minimal in
D
,.
Then, one can obtain
\widehat{D}
from
D
by contraction, such that
either

1.

\widehat{D}
contains an ordered
t
-tough-pair, or

2.

\widehat{D}^{*}
contains a
t
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal in
\widehat{D}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

We now give a proof of Lemma
4.1
using the lemmas stated earlier. The proofs of the those lemmas appear in their respective sections.

Set
t_{1}=\max\{\textnormal{{cor}}(9t),9t\}
,
t_{2}=\max\{f(t_{1},2t_{1}+2),\textnormal{{cor}}(9t_{1}),9t_{1}\}
where
f
is defined as in Lemma
4.9
and
cor
is defined in Corollary
4.7
. Then, define
t^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}(2t_{2},9)
. We will now show that if
D
contains a
t^{\prime}
-tough-pair, then there exists
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
that is a
t
-hard-pattern.

Given
D
, let
(X,Y)
be a
t^{\prime}
-tough-pair in
D
. Let
D_{1}
be a spanning subgraph of
D
such that
D_{1}
is reachability-minimal,
D_{1}
is transitively equivalent to
D
and
(X,Y)
is a
t^{\prime}
-tough-pair in
D_{1}
. Observe that such a graph
D_{1}
exists and can be obtained by starting with the edge set
E_{0}=X\cup Y
and adding an edge of
D
to
E_{0}
as long as
D[E_{0}]
remains reachability-minimal.

Using Lemma
4.3
on
D_{1}
, we conclude that either
D_{1}
contains an ordered
t_{2}
-tough-pair, or a
t_{2}
-biclique, or there exists
A,B\subseteq V(D_{1})
such that
|A|=|B|=t_{2}
and
D_{1}
contains an
(A,B)
-induced-matching and
D_{1}^{\star}
contains an
(A,B)
-induced-biclique.

In the first case, by applying Corollary
4.7
on
D^{\star}_{1}
, we either get
D_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
such that
D_{2}
is some
t
-hard-matching-pattern,
or conclude that
D^{\star}_{1}
contains a
9t
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal in
D_{1}
. Then applying Lemma
4.8
to
D_{1}
gives some
t
-hard-biclique-pattern in
\mathcal{D}
.
In the second case, again by applying Lemma
4.8
to
D_{1}
gives some
t
-hard-biclique-pattern in
\mathcal{D}
.

In the third case, by applying Lemma
4.9
we conclude that there exists
D_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
such that either
D_{2}
contains an ordered
t_{1}
-tough-pair, in which case a further application of Corollary
4.7
on
D^{\star}_{2}
either yields a
D_{3}\in\mathcal{D}
which is a
t
-hard-matching-pattern, or we conclude that
D^{\star}_{2}
contains a
9t
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal in
D_{2}
. In the remaining cases, applying Lemma
4.8
yields a
D_{4}\in\mathcal{D}
which is some
t
-hard-biclique-pattern.
∎

Basic terminology for the remaining section.

Throughout the remaining section, we use the following basic notation. For two integers
i,j
,
[i]
denotes the set
\{1,\ldots,i\}
and
[i,j]
denotes the set
\{i,i+1,\ldots,j\}
. For any (di)graph
D
and sets
S,T\subseteq V(D)
, an
(S,T)
-path in
D
is a path in
D
from some vertex of
S
to some vertex of
T
. If
S
or
T
is singleton, say
S=\{v\}
, then we use the notation
(v,T)
-path. Let
e\in E(D)
, let
D/e
represents the (di)graph obtained after contracting
e
, that is, by deleting the endpoints of
e
and adding a new vertex
x_{e}
such that the set of in-neighbours (resp. out-neighbours) of
x_{e}
is the union of the set of in-neighbours (resp. out-neighbours) of the end points of
e
in
D
. For
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(D)
,
V(E^{\prime})\subseteq V(G)
is the set of vertices that are endpoints of some edge in
E^{\prime}
. If
D
is a digraph, the for any
v\in V(D)
,
N^{-}_{D}(v)
denotes the set of in-neighbours if
v
in
D
,
N^{+}_{D}(v)
denotes the set of out-neighbours if
v
in
D
and
N_{D}(v)
denotes the set of in-neighbours and out-neighbours, called the neighbours, of
v
in
D
.
We say that a digraph
D
is connected if its underlying undirected graph is connected.

4.1
Simplifying the
t
-tough-pair

In this section we prove Lemma
4.3
restated below.
See
4.3

Proof.

Let
(E_{1},E_{2})
be an
\mathcal{R}(2t,9s)
-tough-pair in
D
.
Let
t^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}(2t,9)
.
Fix an arbitrary ordering of the edges of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
. Let
E_{1}=(e^{1}_{1},\ldots,e^{1}_{t^{\prime}})
and let
E_{2}=(e^{2}_{1},\ldots,e^{2}_{t^{\prime}})
.
Fix
i\in[2]
. We will use Ramsey arguments to prove the lemma. Towards this, construct an auxiliary undirected, complete, edge-colored graph
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
as follows. The vertex set of
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
corresponds to the edges of
E_{i}
, that is, for each
e\in E_{i}
there is a vertex corresponding to
e
.
For the sake of simplicity we denote the vertex of
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
that correspond to the edge
e
of
E_{i}
, by
e
itself.
The coloring function
\texttt{col}_{i}
on the edges of
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
(equivalently on the pair of distinct edges of
E_{i}
) is defined based on the following. Fix any two distinct edges
e=(u,v),e^{\prime}=(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in E_{i}
. The coloring function
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})
is defined based on the existence of the edges
(u,v^{\prime})
and
(u^{\prime},v)
in
D
and
D^{\star}
. Below we describe
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})
.

1.

If
(u,v^{\prime})\in E(D)
, and

(a)

(u^{\prime},v)\in E(D)
, then
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})=(1,1)
,

(b)

(u^{\prime},v)\in E(D^{\star})\setminus E(D)
, then
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})=(1,2)
,

(c)

(u^{\prime},v)\not\in E(D)
,
(u^{\prime},v)\not\in E(D^{\star})
, then
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})=(1,3)
.

2.

If
(u,v^{\prime})\in E(D^{\star})\setminus E(D)
, and

(a)

(u^{\prime},v)\in E(D)
, then
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})=(2,1)
,

(b)

(u^{\prime},v)\in E(D^{\star})\setminus E(D)
, then
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})=(2,2)
,

(c)

(u^{\prime},v)\not\in E(D)
,
(u^{\prime},v)\not\in E(D^{\star})
, then
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})=(2,3)
.

3.

If
(u,v^{\prime})\not\in E(D)
,
(u,v^{\prime})\not\in E(D^{\star})
, and

(a)

(u^{\prime},v)\in E(D)
, then
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})=(3,1)
,

(b)

(u^{\prime},v)\in E(D^{\star})\setminus E(D)
, then
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})=(3,2)
,

(c)

(u^{\prime},v)\not\in E(D)
,
(u^{\prime},v)\not\in E(D^{\star})
, then
\texttt{col}_{i}(e,e^{\prime})=(3,3)
.

We now use a Ramsey argument on the
t^{\prime}
-vertex graph
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
with edge-coloring function
\texttt{col}_{i}
(that uses at most
9
different colors). Using Ramsey arguments, we conclude that there exists a monochromatic clique in
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
of size
2t
. Below we show how one can get one of the three outcomes in the lemma statement based of the color of the monochromatic clique. Say edges of
E_{i}
that correspond to this monochromatic clique in
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
are

E^{\prime}_{i}=\{e^{i}_{j_{1}},e^{i}_{j_{2}},\ldots,e^{i}_{j_{t}}\}
such that
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{t}
(recall we fixed an ordering of the edges of
E_{i}
). For the ease of notation later, we assume that the sets
E^{\prime}_{i}
are ordered with the ordering as described in the previous line.

Claim 4.10
.

If for each
i\in[2]
, the color of the monochromatic clique in
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
is
(j,3)
or
(3,j)
for any
j\in[3]
, then
(E^{\prime}_{1},E^{\prime}_{2})
form an ordered
2t
-tough-pair in
D
.

Proof.

Since
(E_{1},E_{2})
is a tough-pair and
E^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq E_{i}
,
(E^{\prime}_{1},E^{\prime}_{2})
is also a tough-pair (of size
t
). If
\texttt{col}_{i}
colors the clique corresponding to the edges of
E^{\prime}_{i}
with the color
(j,3)
, then consider the ordering
(e^{i}_{j_{1}},e^{i}_{j_{2}},\ldots,e^{i}_{j_{t}})
of
E^{\prime}_{i}
, otherwise (when
\texttt{col}_{i}
colors the monochromatic clique with color
(3,j)
), then consider the ordering
(e^{i}_{j_{t}},\ldots,e^{i}_{j_{1}})
of
E^{\prime}_{i}
. Then from the description of the definition of
\texttt{col}_{i}
corresponding to the case when
\texttt{col}_{i}
takes value
(3,j)
or
(j,3)
, one concludes that
(E^{\prime}_{1},E^{\prime}_{2})
is indeed an ordered
t
-tough-pair with the ordering described above.
∎

Note that if the graph has an ordered
2t
-tough-pair, then it also has an ordered
t
-tough-pair.

Observation 4.11
.

It is easy to observe that if the color of the monochromatic clique in
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
is
(1,1)
, then
(\text{tail}(E^{\prime}_{i}),\text{head}(E^{\prime}_{i}))
form a
2t
-biclique in
D
. If the color is
(2,2)
, then
D
contains the
(\text{tail}(E^{\prime}_{i}),\text{head}(E^{\prime}_{i}))
-induced matching and
D^{\star}
contains the
(\text{tail}(E^{\prime}_{i}),\text{head}(E^{\prime}_{i}))
-biclique (thus, the third outcome of the lemma holds).

Note that if the graph has
2t
-biclique, then it also has an ordered
t
-biclique.

Claim 4.12
.

If there exists
i\in[2]
such that the color of the monochromatic clique in
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
is
(1,2)
or
(2,1)
, then
H
contains a
t
-biclique.

Proof.

Suppose that the color of the monochromatic clique in
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
is
(1,2)
. Recall that
E^{\prime}_{i}=((e^{i}_{j_{1}},e^{i}_{j_{2}},\ldots,e^{i}_{j_{t}}))
is the ordered set of edges that correspond to the monochromatic clique. The
(\text{tail}(\{e^{i}_{j_{1}},\ldots,\allowbreak e^{i}_{j_{t}}\}),\allowbreak\text{head}(\{e^{i}_{j_{t+1}},\ldots,\allowbreak e^{i}_{j_{2t}}\}))
form a
t
-biclique in
D
.

Similarly, if the color of the monochromatic clique in
\textnormal{{Aux}}_{i}
is
(2,1)
, then
(\text{tail}(\{e^{i}_{j_{t+1}},\ldots,\allowbreak e^{i}_{j_{2t}}\}),\allowbreak\text{head}(\{e^{i}_{j_{1}},\ldots,\allowbreak e^{i}_{j_{t}}\}))
form a
t
-biclique in
H
.
∎

Thus, from Claims
4.12
and
4.10
, and Observation
4.11
, the lemma follows.
∎

4.2
Simplifying a biclique

In this section, we prove the following lemma. Recall that, from Section
4
, for positive integer
t,p
,
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(t)
is the smallest positive integer such that any bipartite graph with a matching of size
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(t)
either has an induced matching of size
t
or a
t
-induced-biclique.
Furethermore
f(t,p)=2^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(2t)}f(t,p-1)+\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(2t)

when
p>1
and
f(t,1)=t
.

See
4.9

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of Lemma
4.9
. Recall that
A,B
are ordered sets. Let
g(t)=f(t,\mathcal{R}(2t,2))
.
Further let
A=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{g(t)})
and
B=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{g(t)})
.

We begin by showing that, one can assume without loss of generality that
D
is acyclic.
Suppose that
D
is not acyclic. First observe that for any
a_{i},a_{j}\in A
, such that
i\neq j
,
a_{i}
and
a_{j}
belong to different strongly connected components of
D
. Similarly, each vertex of
B
belongs to a distinct strongly connected component of
D
. Further, for any
a_{i},b_{j}
,
a_{i}
and
b_{j}
do not belong to the same strongly connected component of
D
. Indeed, as otherwise there is a
(b_{j},a_{i})
-path in
D
contracting the definition of an
(A,B)
-biclique. Thus, each vertex in
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{g(t)},b_{1},\ldots,b_{g(t)}\}
belong to distinct strongly connected components of
D
.
Let
D^{\prime}
be obtained from
D
by contracting each strongly connected component of
D
into a single vertex. That is,
D^{\prime}
has a vertex for each strongly connected component of
D
and for two vertices
u,v
in
D^{\prime}
, there is an edge from
u
to
v
in
D^{\prime}
if there is vertex in the strongly connected component of
D
which was contracted onto
u
, that has an edge in
D
to a vertex of the strongly connected component of
D
which was contracted to
v
. Let us call a vertex of
D^{\prime}

a_{i}
if it is obtained by contracting a strongly connected component containing
a_{i}
. Similarly, let us call a vertex of
D^{\prime}

b_{i}
if it is obtained by contracting a strongly connected component containing
b_{i}
. It is easy to observe that
D^{\prime}
also contains an
(A,B)
-matching and
D^{\prime\star}
contains an
(A,B)
-induced-biclique. Indeed, since an
(a_{i},b_{j})
-path in
D
implies an
(a_{i},b_{j})
-path in
D^{\prime}
and no
(a_{i},a_{j})
-path (resp. no
(b_{i},b_{j})
-path) in
D
implies no
(a_{i},a_{j})
-path (resp. no
(b_{i},b_{j})
-path) in
D^{\prime}
. Further if
D^{\prime}
contains an
(a_{i},b_{i})
-path of length strictly greater than
1
then so does
D
. Since
D^{\prime}
is obtained by contraction operation from
D
, we conclude that without loss of generality, we can assume for the rest of the section that the input graph
D
in Lemma
4.9
is acyclic.

We say that an edge
e\in E(D)\setminus E(A,B)
is
contraction-redundant
with respect to
(A,B)
in
D
if the following holds.

1.

If
D/e
is acyclic,

2.

all the edges in
\{(a_{i},b_{i}):i\in[g(t)]\}
(that is the matching edges of
(A,B)
) are minimal edges in
D/e
, and

3.

(D/e)^{\star}
has an
(A,B)
-induced-biclique..

Let
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
be the graph obtained from
D
by repeatedly contracting the contraction-redundant edges with respect to
(A,B)
until
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
has no such edge.
For the ease of notation by
aa
-path we mean a path between two distinct vertices of
A
, by
bb
-path we mean a path between two distinct vertices of
B
and by
a_{\ast}b_{\ast}
-path we mean a
(a_{i},b_{i})
-path for some
i\in[g(t)]
.
We divide the proof of Lemma
4.9
into two independent parts based on the length of a longest path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. In Section
4.2.1
, we consider the case when the length of a longest path in
D_{contr}
is at least
2t+2
.
In this case, we show that
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
contains an ordered
t
-tough-pair.
In Section
4.2.2
, we consider the case when the length of any longest path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
is at most
2t+2
. In this case, we get one of the desired outputs.

4.2.1
When
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
has a long path

In this section, we prove Lemma
4.9
when the length of a longest path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
is at least
2t+2
.
Let
P=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{p})

be a directed longest path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
(then
p\geq 2t+2
).
Recall that none of the edges of
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, and in particular, none of the edges of
P
, are
contraction-redundant
with respect to
(A,B)
.

Lemma 4.13
.

For each
e\in E(P)
,
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}/e
is acyclic.

Proof.

Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that there is a cycle in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}/e
. Let
e=(v_{i},v_{i+1})
.
Then, either there is a
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}-\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\}
or a
(v_{i+1},v_{i})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
If there exists a
(v_{i+1},v_{i})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
then together with the edge
e=(v_{i},v_{i+1})
, it creates a directed closed walk in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
This contradicts that
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
is acyclic.

In the other case, suppose there exists a
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
-path, say
P^{\prime}
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}-\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\}
. We first claim that the internal vertices of
P^{\prime}
are disjoint from
P
. Suppose not, let
v_{j}\in V(P)
be the first internal vertex of
P^{\prime}
that belongs to
P
. Then there is a
(v_{i},v_{j})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
and
(v_{j},v_{i+1})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. If
j<i
, then the
(v_{i},v_{j})
-path together with the
(v_{j},v_{i})
-subpath of
P
, gives a closed walk in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, contradicting its acyclicity. Otherwise,
j>i+1
. In this case, the
(v_{j},v_{i+1})
-path together with the
(v_{i+1},v_{j})
-subpath of
P
gives a closed walk in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, again contradicting its acyclicity.
∎

Since each edge of
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, in particular, each edge of
P
, is not contraction-redundant, from Lemma
4.13
and the definition of contraction-redundant, for each
e\in E(P)
either

1.

there exists an
aa
-path, or a
bb
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}/e
(that is,
(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}/e)^{\star}
does not contain the
(A,B)
-induced-biclique), or

2.

there exists
i\in[g(t)]
an
a_{i}b_{i}
-path in
(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}/e)-\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}
(
(a_{i},b_{i})
is not a minimal edge in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}/e
).

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
e=(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E(P)
such that
H_{contr}/e
has an
(a_{j},b_{j})
-path. Then there exists an
(a_{j},v_{i+1})
-path and a
(v_{i},b_{j})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

If
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}/e
has a
(a_{j},a_{\ell})
-path, for some
a_{j},a_{\ell}\in A
, then there exists an
(a_{j},v_{i+1})
-path and a
(v_{i},a_{\ell})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

If
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}/e
has a
(b_{j},b_{\ell})
-path, for some
b_{j},b_{\ell}\in B
, then there exists an
(b_{j},v_{i+1})
-path and
(v_{i},b_{\ell})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

Proof.

Let
e=(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E(P)
such that
D_{contr}/e
has an
(a_{j},b_{j})
-path. Then either there exists an
(a_{j},v_{i})
-path and a
(v_{i+1},b_{j})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, or there exists an
(a_{j},v_{i+1})
-path and
(v_{i},b_{j})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. In the later case, we are done. The former case implies an
(a_{j},b_{j})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
of length at least three (with at least three edges), which contradicts that
(a_{j},b_{j})
is a minimal edge in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

If
D_{contr}/e
has an
(a_{j},a_{\ell})
-path, then either there exists an
(a_{j},v_{i})
-path and a
(v_{i+1},a_{\ell})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, or there exists an
(a_{j},v_{i+1})
-path and
(v_{i},a_{\ell})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. In the later case, we are done. The former case implies an
(a_{j},a_{\ell})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
which contradicts that
(A,B)
is an induced biclique in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
(in particular, that
A
is an independent set in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
).
One can similarly show that if
D_{contr}/e
has a
(b_{j},b_{\ell})
-path, then there exists an
(b_{j},v_{i+1})
-path and
(v_{i},b_{\ell})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
∎

For each
e=(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E(P)
, we say that:

1.

e
is
j
-irredundant
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, if there is an
(a_{j},v_{i+1})
-path and a
(v_{i},b_{j})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

2.

e
is
aa
-irredundant
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, if there exists
a_{j},a_{\ell}\in A
such that there is an
(a_{j},v_{i+1})
-path and a
(v_{i},a_{\ell})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

3.

e
is
bb
-irredundant
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, if there exists
b_{j},b_{\ell}\in B
such that there is an
(b_{j},v_{i+1})
-path and
(v_{i},b_{\ell})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

From Lemmas
4.13
and
4.18
, for each
e\in E(P)
, either
e
is
j
-irredundant, for some
j\in[g(t)]
, or is
aa
-irredundant or is
bb
-irredundant.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
e=(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E(P)
such that
e
is
aa
-irredundant in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Then
i=1
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose that
i>1
. Let
e^{\prime}=(v_{i-1},v_{i})
.
If
e^{\prime}
is
aa
-irredundant or
e^{\prime}
is
j
-irredundant for some
j\in[g(t)]
, then there exists a path from some vertex of
A
to
v_{i}
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Also since
e
is
aa
-irredundant, there exists a path from
v_{i}
to some vertex of
A
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. This implies either a directed closed walk in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, contradicting its acyclicity, or a path between two distinct vertices of
A
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, contradicting that
A
is an independent set in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
.

If
e^{\prime}
is
bb
-irredundant, then there exists a path from some vertex of
B
to
v_{i}
. Also since
e
is
aa
-irredundant, there exists a path from
v_{i}
to some vertex of
A
. This implies a path from some vertex of
B
to some vertex of
A
. Since
(A,B)
is an induced biclique in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
, that is there is a path from every vertex of
A
to every vertex of
B
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, this implies a directed closed walk in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, contradicting its acyclicity.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
e=(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E(P)
such that
e
is
bb
-irredundant in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Then
i+1=p
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose that
i+1<p
. Let
e^{\prime}=(v_{i+1},v_{i+2})
.
If
e^{\prime}
is
bb
-irredundant or
e^{\prime}
is
j
-irredundant for some
j\in[g(t)]
, then there exists a path from
v_{i+1}
to some vertex of
B
. Also since
e
is
bb
-irredundant, there exists a path from some vertex of
B
to
v_{i+1}
. This either implies a directed closed walk in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, contradicting its acyclicity, or a path between two distinct vertices of
B
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, contradicting that
B
is an independent set in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
.

If
e^{\prime}
is
aa
-irredundant, then there exists a path from
v_{i+1}
to some vertex of
A
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Also since
e
is
bb
-irredundant, there exists a path from some vertex of
B
to
v_{i+1}
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. This implies a path from some vertex of
B
to some vertex of
A
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Since

(A,B)
is an induced-biclique in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
, this implies a directed closed walk
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, contradicting its acyclicity.
∎

Let
P^{\prime}
be a subpath obtained from
P
by without the first and last edge of
P
, that is
P^{\prime}=(v_{2},\ldots,v_{p-1})
. From Lemmas
4.15
and
4.16
, each edge
e\in E(P^{\prime})
is
j
-irredundant for some
j\in[g(t)]
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
e,e^{\prime}
be distinct edges of
P^{\prime}
such that
e
is
q
-irredundant in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
and
e^{\prime}
is
r
-irredundant in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, for some
q,r\in[g(t)]
. Then
q\neq r
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose that
q=r
. Without loss of generality, let
e=(v_{i},v_{i+1})
and
e^{\prime}=(v_{j},v_{j+1})
such that
i<j
. Then there exists an
(a_{q},v_{i+1})
-path and
(v_{j},b_{q})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Since
i<j
, there exists a path from
(v_{i},v_{j})
(which is the the
(v_{i+1},v_{j})
-subpath of
P^{\prime}
) in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. This implies an
(a_{q},b_{q})
-path of length strictly greater than one in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, contradicting that
(a_{q},b_{q})
is a minimal edge in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
∎

For each
e=(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E(P^{\prime})
, fix a
\rho(i)\in[g(t)]
, such
e
is
\rho(i)
-irredundant in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
The following observation follows from Lemma
4.17
.

Observation 4.18
.

For each
v_{i}\in V(P^{\prime})
,
i\in[3,p-2]
, there exists a
(a_{\rho(i-1)},v_{i})
-path and a
(v_{i},b_{\rho(i)})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Also, for
i,j\in[3,p-2]
,
i\neq j
,
\rho(i)\neq\rho(j)
.

For each
i\in[3,p-2]
, let
P^{\texttt{a}}_{i}
denote an (arbitrarily) fixed
(a_{\rho(i-1)},v_{i})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
and let
P^{\texttt{b}}_{i}
denote an (arbitrarily) fixed
(v_{i},b_{\rho(i)})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
Let
E_{1}=(v_{3},b_{\rho(3)}),\allowbreak(v_{4},b_{\rho(4)}),\ldots,\allowbreak(v_{p/2-2},b_{\rho(p/2-2)})
and let

E_{2}=((a_{\rho(p/2-2)},v_{p/2-1}),\allowbreak(a_{\rho(p/2-1)},\allowbreak v_{p/2}),\ldots,(a_{\rho(p-1)},\allowbreak v_{p-2})
.
From Observation
4.18
, each edge of
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
is an edge in
D^{\star}_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
and the endpoints of the edges in
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
are distinct. We will now show that
(E_{1},E_{2})
is an ordered
(p/2-1)
-tough-pair in
D^{\star}_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Since
p\geq 2t+2
, this proves Lemma
4.9
in the case when the length of a longest path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
is at least
2t+2
.

Lemma 4.19
.

(E_{1},E_{2})
is an ordered
(p/2-1)
-tough-pair in
D^{\star}_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

Proof.

To prove the lemma, we prove the following claims.

Claim 4.20

(Weak independence of
E_{1}
(resp.
E_{2}
))
.

The edges in
E_{1}
are pairwise weakly independent. Similarly, the edges in
E_{2}
are pairwise weakly independent in
D^{\star}_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

Proof.

Let
(a_{\rho(i-1)},{v}_{i}),\allowbreak(a_{\rho(j-1)},{v}_{j})\in E_{1}
. For the sake of contradiction, say
(v_{j},a_{\rho(i-1)})\in D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
. Then this implies a
(a_{\rho(j-1},a_{\rho(i-1)})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
contradicting that
A
is an independent set in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
.
Using symmetric arguments one can show that all edges in
E_{2}
are pairwise weakly independent.
∎

Claim 4.21

(Orderedness on
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
)
.

For any
i,j\in[3,p-2]
,
i<j
,
(a_{\rho(j-1)},v_{i}),\allowbreak(a_{\rho(j-1)},a_{\rho(i-1)}),\allowbreak({v}_{j},\allowbreak b_{\rho(i)}),\allowbreak(v_{j},v_{i})\not\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
.

Proof.

Since
A
is an independent set in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
,
(a_{\rho(j-1)},a_{\rho(i-1)})\not\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
. Since
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
is acyclic and there is a
(v_{i},v_{j})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, we conclude that
(v_{j},v_{i})\not\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
.
If there is an
(a_{\rho(j-1)},{v}_{i})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, then this together with the
({v}_{i},v_{j-1})
-subpath of
P^{\prime}
and the
(v_{i-1},b_{\rho(i-1)})
path
P^{\texttt{b}}_{i-1}
, implies a
(a_{\rho(j-1),b_{\rho(j-1)}})
-path of length strictly greater than
1
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, which is a contradiction. Using symmetric arguments, one can show that

({v}_{j},b_{\rho(i)}),(v_{j},v_{i})\not\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
.
∎

Claim 4.22

(Weak independence between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
)
.

Let
(v_{i},b_{\rho(i)})\in E_{1}
and
(a_{\rho(j-1)},v_{j})\in E_{2}
.
There is
(v_{j},b_{\rho(i)}),(b_{\rho(i)},a_{\rho(i-1)})\not\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
.

Proof.

First observe from the construction of
E_{1},E_{2}
that
i<j
.
If
(v_{j},b_{\rho(i)})\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
, then the
(a_{\rho(i)},v_{i+1})
-path
P^{\texttt{a}}_{i+1}
, together with the
(v_{i+1},v_{j})
-subpath of
P^{\prime}
, together with the
(v_{j},b_{\rho(i)})
-path, implies a
(a_{\rho(i)},b_{\rho(i)})
-path of length strictly greater than
1
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, which is a contradiction.

If
(b_{\rho(i)},a_{\rho(i-1)})\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
, then the
(a_{rho(i-1)},b_{\rho(i)})
-path obtained by concatenating
P^{\texttt{a}}_{i}
and
P^{\texttt{b}}_{i}
, together with the
(b_{\rho(i)},a_{\rho(i-1)})
-path, implies a
(a_{\rho(i-1)},a_{\rho(j-1)})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, which contradicts that
A
is an independent set in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
∎

Claim 4.23

(Strong independence between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
)
.

Let
(v_{i},b_{\rho(i)})\in E_{1}
and
(a_{\rho(j-1)},v_{j})\in E_{2}
.
Then
(v_{i},a_{\rho(j-1}),\allowbreak(a_{\rho(j-1)},v_{i}),\allowbreak(b_{\rho(i)},v_{j}),\allowbreak(v_{j},b_{\rho(i)})\not\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
.

Proof.

Recall that
i>j
.
If
(v_{i},a_{\rho(j-1)})\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
, then together with
P^{\texttt{a}}_{i}
, it contradicts that
A
is independent in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
. Similarly, if
(b_{\rho(i)},v_{j})\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
, then it contradicts the independence of
B
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
.

If
(a_{\rho(j-1)},v_{i})\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
, then this together with the
(v_{i},v_{j-1})
-subpath of
P^{\prime}
, together with the path
P^{\texttt{b}}_{j-1}
, implies a
(a_{\rho(j-1)},b_{\rho(j-1)})
-path of length strictly greater than
1
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, which is a contradiction. Similarly if,
(v_{j},b_{\rho(i)})\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
, then this implies a
(a_{\rho(i)},b_{\rho(i)})
-path of length strictly greater than
1
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

This concludes the proof.
∎

4.2.2
No long path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}

In this section, we prove Lemma
4.9
in the case when the length of a longest path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
is
p\leq 2t+2
. Recall that
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
contains
(A,B)
-matching and
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
contains
(A,B)
-induced-biclique. Also, the edges of
A\cup B
are minimal in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. We prove Lemma
4.9
by induction on the length of a longest
(A,B)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
For this purpose we essentially restate Lemma
4.9
in a form that is “induction-friendly”. Observe, as a base case, that when the length of a longest
(A,B)
-path is
1
, then since
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
contains
(A,B)
-induced-biclique, we conclude that
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
contains
(A,B)
-induced-biclique and the edges of the
(A,B)
-induced-biclique are minimal. In this case, we conclude that we get the second output of Lemma
4.9
.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
A,B\subseteq V(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}})
be ordered sets such that the length
of a longest
(A,B)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
is
p
,

|A|=|B|\geq f(t,p)
,
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
contains an
(A,B)
-induced-biclique and the matching edges of
(A,B)
are minimal in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, then either

1.

there exist ordered sets
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\subseteq V(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}})
such that the length
of a longest
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
is at most
p
,

|A^{\prime}|=|B^{\prime}|\geq f(t,p-1)
,
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
contains an
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
-induced-biclique and the matching edges of
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
are minimal in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, or

2.

D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
contains an ordered
t
-tough-pair, or

3.

D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
contains a
t
-induced-biclique whose edges are minimal in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

Proof.

Let
L
be the set of vertices of
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
that contains the first internal vertex on every longest
(A,B)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

Claim 4.25
.

There is no
(L,L)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose there exist
x,y\in L
such that there this an
(x,y)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Note that
x\neq y
, as otherwise,
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
would contain a cycle.
From the definition of
L
, there exists
a_{i},a_{j}\in L
(
i
not necessarily distinct from
j
) such that
x
is the first internal vertex on some
(a_{i},B)
-path, say
P_{x}
, of length
p
and
y
is the first internal vertex on some
(a_{j},B)
-path, say
P_{y}
, of length
p
.
Let
P_{xy}
be some
(x,y)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. We first claim that the set of internal vertices of
P_{x,y}
is disjoint from
P_{y}
. Suppose not. Let
z
be some internal vertex of
P_{xy}
that is also a vertex of
P_{y}
. Since
z\in V(P_{y})
, there exists a
(y,z)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Also since
z\in V(P_{x,y})
, there exists a
(z,y)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. This implies a cycle in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, which is a contradiction. Thus, we conclude that the internal vertices of
P_{xy}
are disjoint from that of
P_{y}
. Then consider the
(x,B)
-path obtained by appending
P_{xy}
and
P_{y}
. Note that the length of
P_{y}
is
p-1
and the length of
P_{xy}
is at least
1
. This
(x,B)
-path, together with the edge
(a_{i},x)
, gives an
(a_{i},B)
-path of length strictly greater than
p
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
, which contradicts that the length of a longest
(A,B)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
is
p
.
∎

Claim 4.26
.

For each
(a,x)\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}})
such that
a\in A
and
x\in L
,
(a,x)
is a minimal edge of
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, say there exists an
(a,x)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
of length at least
2
. Let this path be
P_{ax}
. Since
x\in L
, there exists an
(A,B)
-path of length
p
whose first internal vertex is
x
. Let this path be
P_{AB}
. Then the internal vertices of
P_{ax}
are disjoint from the internal vertices of
P_{AB}
, as otherwise there would be a cycle in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. By appending the path
P_{sx}
(which is of length at least
2
) with the subpath of
P_{ABs}
starting from
x
(which is of length
p-1
), we get an
(A,B)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
of length at least
p+1
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Claim 4.27
.

Let
a\in A
and
x\in L
, such that
(a,x)\not\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}})
. Then
(a,x)\not\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star})
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose there exists an
(a,x)
-path, say
P_{a,x}
in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
of length at least two. Since
x\in L
, there exists
a^{\prime}\in A
(
a^{\prime}
not necessarily distinct from
a
) such that
x
is the first internal vertex of a
(a^{\prime},T)
-path, say
P_{a^{\prime}B}
, of length
p
. First observe that the internal vertices of
P_{a,x}
are disjoint from that of
P_{a^{\prime}B}
, as otherwise there would be a cycle in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. The path
P_{a,x}
appended with the
(x,B)
-subpath of
P_{a^{\prime}B}
gives an
(A,B)
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
of length strictly greater than
p
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Consider the bipartite graph
D_{\texttt{bip}}=D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}[A\cup L]
. Note, from Claim
4.26
, that each edge of
D_{\texttt{bip}}
is a minimal edge in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
We now distinguish into two cases based on the size of the matching in
D_{\texttt{bip}}
.

Case 1: The size of a maximum matching in
D_{\texttt{bip}}
is at least
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(2t)
.
In this case, from Ramsey’s Theorem, either there exists an induced matching of size
2t
in
D_{\texttt{bip}}
or a
2t
-induced-biclique. From Claim
4.26
, each edge of
D_{\texttt{bip}}
is a minimal edge of
D_{contr}
.

In the first case, we get sets
A^{*}\subseteq A
and
B^{*}\subseteq L
such that there is an
(A^{*},B^{*})
-induced-matching in
D_{\texttt{bip}}
. Since
A
is an independent set in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
, so is
A^{*}
. Since
B^{*}\subseteq L
, from Claim
4.26
,
B^{*}
is an independent set in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
. Thus,
(A^{*},B^{*})
is an induced-matching in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Further, from Claim
4.27
,
(A^{*},B^{*})
is in fact an induced matching in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}^{\star}
. Observe that if a digraph has an induced-matching of size
2t
in its transitive closure, then it has a ordered
t
-tough-pair. Thus, in this case we get the second outcome of the lemma.

In the second case, when
H_{\texttt{bip}}
contains a
2t
-induced-biclique, then this is also a
2t
-induced-biclique in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
where all edges of the biclique are minimal edges of
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Thus, in this case we get the third outcome of the lemma.

Case 2: The size of a maximum matching in
D_{\texttt{bip}}
is at most
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(2t)
.
In this case,
we will find ordered sets
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\subseteq V(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}})
satisfies the properties stated in the first outcome of the lemma.

Let
Z
be a minimum vertex cover of
D_{\texttt{bip}}
of size at most
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(2t)
. Let
Z_{A}=Z\cap A
and

Z_{L}=Z\cap L
. Let
A^{*}=A\setminus Z_{S}
. Since
Z_{A}\cup Z_{L}
is a vertex cover of
D_{\texttt{bip}}
, for any
a\in A^{*}
,
N_{L}(a)\subseteq Z_{L}
.
For each subset
L^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{L}
, let
A_{L^{\prime}}\subseteq A^{*}
such that for each vertex
a\in A_{L^{\prime}}
,
N_{Z_{L}}(a)=L^{\prime}
(and hence
N_{L}(a)=L^{\prime}
).

Fix
L^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{L}
such that
|S_{L^{\prime}}|
is maximized.
Recall that the sets
A=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{f(t,p)})
and
T=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{f(t,p)})
are ordered sets.
Set
A^{\prime}=A_{L^{\prime}}
and
B^{\prime}
be the corresponding vertices of
B
,
that is
b_{i}\in B^{\prime}
if and only if
a_{i}\in A^{\prime}
.
From the choice of
L^{\prime}
,
|A^{\prime}|=|B^{\prime}|\geq(|A|-\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(2t))/2^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(2t)}=f(t,p-1)
.
We will now show that the length of a longest
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
is at most
p-1
.

Claim 4.28
.

Let
a_{i}\in A^{\prime}
and
b_{j}\in B^{\prime}
such that
i\neq j
. Let
P=(a_{i},x_{1},\ldots,x_{q},b_{j})
be a longest
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
. Then
x_{1}\not\in L
.

Proof.

Since
b_{j}\in B^{\prime}
,
a_{j}\in A^{\prime}
(from construction of
B^{\prime}
).
First observe that since
b_{j}\in B^{\prime}
, then
a_{j}\in A^{\prime}
.
For the sake of contradiction, suppose
x_{1}\in L
. Since
(a_{i},x)\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}})
and all the vertices of
A^{\prime}
have the same neighbourhood in
Z_{L}
and
a_{j}\in A^{\prime}
, we conclude that
(a_{j},x)\in E(D_{\textnormal{{contr}}})
. Thus, there is an
(a_{j},b_{j})
-path in
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
of length strictly greater than one, which contradicts that
(a_{j},b_{j})
is a minimal edge of
D_{\textnormal{{contr}}}
.
∎

From Claim
4.28
, the first internal vertex of every longest
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
-path is not contained in
L
. Now suppose that there exists an
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
-path of length
p
. Then its first internal vertex should be in
L
by the definition of
L
. This is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma
4.24
proves Lemma
4.9
when the length of the longest path
p\leq 2t+2
.

4.3
Cleaning the ordered tough-pair

The cleaning of ordered tough-pairs is done in two steps:
we first show that an ordered tough-pair can be identified to a so-called
semi-cleaned ordered tough-pair
(see
Section

4.3.1
) and show thereafter how to further identify a semi-cleaned ordered tough-pair to a hard matching pattern (see
Section

4.3.2
).

4.3.1
Semi-cleaning

Recall the definition of a semi-cleaned ordered
t
-tough-pair from Section
4
.
The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
4.5
restated below.

See
4.5

To prove Lemma
4.5
, we first define a type of a vertex in
D
with respect to the ordered
t
-tough-pair
(A,B)
. This is based on the neighbourhoods of a vertex in the head sets and tail sets of
A
and
B
. We then define a notion of a bad type. The vertices having a bad type hinder the semi-cleaning procedure. To overcome this,
we show that if a digraph
D
contains an ordered
t^{2}
-tough-pair then it also contains an ordered
t
-tough-pair
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
such that there are no bad vertices with respect to
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
. We then show that given such an ordered tough-pair
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
we can identify the remaining vertices to one of the endpoints of
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}
.

Bad vertices with respect to the ordered tough-pair
(A,B)
.

Given an ordered tough-pair
(A,B)
in a digraph
D
,
we say that a vertex
v\in V(D)\setminus V(A\cup B)
is
bad
with respect to
(A,B)
if
v\in N^{+}_{D^{\star}}(\text{tail}(A))\cap N^{-}_{D^{\star}}(\text{head}(A))\cap N^{+}_{D^{\star}}(\text{tail}(B))\cap N^{-}_{D^{\star}}(\text{head}(B))
.

Lemma 4.29

(Eliminating bad vertices)
.

Let
D
be a
digraph such that
D
contains an ordered
t^{2}
-tough-pair. Then
D
contains an ordered
t
-tough-pair
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
